This tells us that we can write

s_{2}(\gamma(t),\xi)\wedge s_{1}(\gamma(t),\xi)=c\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}

for some constant
c
. In fact, because of the asymptotics of the flat sections, we see that
c=-1
. Hence, putting the results together we get the following asymptotics:

\displaystyle\frac{s_{3}(\gamma(t),\xi)\wedge s_{1}(\gamma(t),\xi)}{s_{2}(\gamma(t),\xi)\wedge s_{1}(\gamma(t),\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad{}\cong_{\xi\to 0,\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\xi}m\operatorname{Log}_{p}\Bigl(-\frac{m}{2e}\Bigr)+{\rm i}m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}_{p}(-m)+{\rm i}\pi+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\right).

So far we have computed the asymptotics as
\xi\to 0
with
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
. Let us compute the asymptotics when
\xi\to 0
with
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}
, and see that they match with the previous asymptotics.

Now we need to compute the asymptotics of

-\frac{1}{b(\xi)}=-\frac{s_{3}(\gamma(t),\xi)\wedge s_{2}(\gamma(t),\xi)}{s_{4}(\gamma(t),\xi)\wedge s_{2}(\gamma(t),\xi)}\qquad\text{where}\quad\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}.

Following a similar computation from before using the relevant expressions for the
s_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
for
i=2,4
, we get the following:

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\cong_{\xi\to 0,\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}}\frac{-1}{\exp\bigl(\frac{1}{\xi}m\operatorname{Log}_{p}\bigl(-\frac{m}{2e}\bigr)-{\rm i}m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}_{p}(-m)-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\bigr)}=A(\xi).

Hence, the asymptotics agree on
\mathbb{H}_{m}
and
\mathbb{H}_{-m}
, and we proved what we want.
∎

3.6.3
The reality condition and the asymptotics as
\boldsymbol{\xi\to\infty}

In this section, we prove the reality condition for the magnetic twistor coordinate (recall (
2.13
)). Once we have the reality condition, the asymptotics when
\xi\to\infty
automatically follow from the asymptotics when
\xi\to 0
.

The reality condition states that the following equality must hold:

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)=\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}}\qquad\text{for}\quad\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}/l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m).

Let
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
be a compatibly framed wild harmonic bundle. We will denote by
(e_{1},e_{2})
an extension of
g
to a
{\rm SU}(2)
frame in a neighborhood
U_{\infty}
of
z=\infty
, where the singularity of the associated flat connection
\nabla^{\xi}=\xi^{-1}\theta+D\bigl(\overline{\partial}_{E},h\bigr)+\xi\theta^{\dagger}
has the appropriate form. We write in this frame
\nabla^{\xi}={\rm d}+A(\xi)
.

We can consider the conjugate bundle
\overline{E}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}
, with the induced connection
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}
. In the induced frame
\{\overline{e}_{i}\}
the connection has the form
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}={\rm d}+\overline{A(\xi)}
. Furthermore, we can consider the dual bundle
\overline{E}^{*}\to\overline{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}
with the induced connection
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}
. In the dual frame
\{\overline{e}^{*}_{i}\}
, the connection
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}
takes the form
{\rm d}-\overline{A(\xi)}^{t}={\rm d}+A\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
.

Consider now the associated compatibly framed meromorphic flat bundle
\bigl(\mathcal{PE}_{a}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{a}^{\xi}\bigr)
and a fundamental solution
(y_{1},y_{2})=\Phi_{i}(\xi)
of
\nabla^{\xi}
on
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i}
, with the corresponding asymptotics determined by the compatible frame. If we denote by
e^{Q(\xi)}=\operatorname{diag}\bigl(e^{Q_{1}(\xi)},e^{Q_{2}(\xi)}\bigr)
, where

\displaystyle e^{Q_{1}(\xi)}=\exp\left(-\xi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2w^{2}}-m\operatorname{Log}(w)\right)-{\rm i}m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}(w)-\xi\left(\frac{1}{2\overline{w}^{2}}-\overline{m}\overline{\operatorname{Log}(w)}\right)\right),

\displaystyle e^{Q_{2}(\xi)}=\exp\left(\xi^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2w^{2}}-m\operatorname{Log}(w)\right)+{\rm i}m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}(w)+\xi\left(\frac{1}{2\overline{w}^{2}}-\overline{m}\overline{\operatorname{Log}(w)}\right)\right),

then we know that by Theorem
3.37
that
(y_{1},y_{2})\cdot e^{-Q(\xi)}\to(e_{1},e_{2})
as
w\to 0
along
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i}
. Here, the
Q_{i}(\xi)
are defined using the same branch of the logarithm and argument as the one used to define the flat frames
\Phi_{i}(\xi)
in Section
3.4.4
. We then have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.61
.

If
(e_{1},e_{2})\cdot B=\Phi_{i}=(y_{1},y_{2})
, then
(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(B^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}:=\overline{\Phi}_{i}^{*}=(\overline{y}_{1}^{*},\overline{y}_{2}^{*})
is a flat frame for
\bigl(\overline{\mathcal{PE}_{a}^{\xi}}^{*},\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}\bigr)
. Furthermore,
(\overline{y}_{1}^{*},\overline{y}_{2}^{*})\cdot e^{-Q(-1/\overline{\xi})}\to(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})
as
w\to 0
along
\widehat{\textnormal{Sect}}_{i}(\xi)
.

Proof.

Since
\Phi_{i}
is a flat frame, and in the frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
we have that
\nabla^{\xi}={\rm d}+A(\xi)
, then
B
must satisfy

B^{-1}{\rm d}B+B^{-1}AB=0
.
It then follows that

-B^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}\bigl(B^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}+B^{\dagger}{\rm d}\bigl(B^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}=0
.
Since in the frame
(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})
, we have that
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}={\rm d}-A^{\dagger}
and conclude that
(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(B^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}
must be a flat frame for
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}
.

To check the asymptotics, just notice that
(y_{1},y_{2})\cdot e^{-Q(\xi)}\to(e_{1},e_{2})
implies that
Be^{-Q(\xi)}\to 1
. On the other hand, we have that

\displaystyle(\overline{y}_{1}^{*},\overline{y}_{2}^{*})\cdot e^{-Q(-1/\overline{\xi})}

\displaystyle=(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(B^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}e^{-Q(-1/\overline{\xi})}=(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(B^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}e^{\overline{Q(\xi)}}

\displaystyle=(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(e^{Q(\xi)}B^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}=(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(\bigl(Be^{-Q(\xi)}\bigr)^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger},

so we conclude that
(\overline{y}_{1}^{*},\overline{y}_{2}^{*})\cdot e^{-Q(-1/\overline{\xi})}\to(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})
as
w\to 0
along
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i}(\xi)
.
∎

We will need two more easy lemmas in order to show the reality condition of the magnetic coordinate.

Lemma 3.62
.

If on
\widehat{\textnormal{Sect}}_{i}\!\cap\!\widehat{\textnormal{Sect}}_{i+1}
, we have
\Phi_{i+1}\!=\!\Phi_{i}\cdot S_{i}
, then we have that
\overline{\Phi}_{i+1}^{*}\!=\!\overline{\Phi}_{i}^{*}\cdot\bigl(S_{i}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

If we write
(e_{1},e_{2})\cdot B_{i}=\Phi_{i}
and
(e_{1},e_{2})\cdot B_{i+1}=\Phi_{i+1}
, then we have that
B_{i}S_{i}=B_{i+1}
. Hence, we get that
\bigl(B_{i}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}\bigl(S_{i}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}=\bigl(B_{i+1}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}
, and then by the previous lemma

\overline{\Phi}_{i+1}^{*}=(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(B_{i+1}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}=(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})\cdot\bigl(B_{i}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}\bigl(S_{i}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}=\overline{\Phi}_{i}^{*}\cdot\bigl(S_{i}^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}.

∎

Lemma 3.63
.

The local bundle map defined by
e_{i}\to\overline{e_{i}}^{*}
, gives an isomorphism
\smash{\bigl(\overline{\mathcal{PE}_{a}^{\xi}}^{*},\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}\bigr)}|_{U_{\infty}}\allowbreak\cong\smash{\bigl(\mathcal{PE}_{a}^{-\frac{1}{\overline{\xi}}},\nabla^{-\frac{1}{\overline{\xi}}}\bigr)}|_{U_{\infty}}
, where
U_{\infty}
is a small neighborhood of
z=\infty
where the frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
is defined. In particular, we have the following correspondence between flat frames: for
i=1,2
and
{\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}}
, the flat frame
\overline{\Phi}_{i}^{*}(\xi)
goes to the flat frame
\Phi_{i+1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
of
\nabla^{-1/\overline{\xi}}
on
\widehat{\textnormal{Sect}}_{i+1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
; and for
{i=2,3}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}
, to the flat frame
\Phi_{i-1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
of
\nabla^{-1/\overline{\xi}}
on
\widehat{\textnormal{Sect}}_{i-1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
.

Proof.

For the first statement, just notice that in the frame
(\overline{e}_{1}^{*},\overline{e}_{2}^{*})
, the induced connection looks like
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}={\rm d}+A\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
. On the other hand, the flat frame
\Phi_{i}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
on
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
is uniquely characterized by the asymptotic condition
\Phi_{i}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)e^{-Q(-1/\overline{\xi})}\to(e_{1},e_{2})
when
{w\to 0}
along
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
. If
i=1,2
and
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
, we have that
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}_{i}}(\xi)=\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i+1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
and that the flat frame
\overline{\Phi}_{i}^{*}
of
\overline{\nabla^{\xi}}^{*}
goes to a flat frame of
\nabla^{-\frac{1}{\overline{\xi}}}
satisfying the corresponding asymptotic condition on
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i+1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
. We then conclude that for
i=1,2
and
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
we have
\overline{\Phi}^{*}_{i}(\xi)=\Phi_{i+1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
. The other case similarly follows.
∎

Theorem 3.64
.

The magnetic twistor coordinate satisfies the following reality condition:

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)=\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}}\qquad\text{for}\quad\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}/l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m).

Proof.

Assume first that
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
. We then want to relate
b\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
and
a(\xi)
.

By the previous two lemmas, we have that

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)\cdot S_{2}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{3}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)=\overline{\Phi}^{*}_{2}(\xi)=\overline{\Phi}^{*}_{1}(\xi)\cdot\bigl(S_{1}(\xi)^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)\cdot\bigl(S_{1}^{-1}(\xi)\bigr)^{\dagger}.

From this, we conclude that
S_{2}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)=\bigl(S_{1}^{-1}(\xi)\bigr)^{\dagger}
, so that the Stokes matrix elements are related by
b\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)=-\overline{a(\xi)}
. Hence, we have that for
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)=a(\xi)=-\overline{b\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)}=\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}}.

Similarly, if
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}
, we need to compare
b(\xi)
and
a\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)
. By the above two lemmas, we have that

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)\cdot S_{1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)=\overline{\Phi}^{*}_{3}(\xi)=\overline{\Phi}^{*}_{2}(\xi)\cdot\bigl(S_{2}(\xi)^{-1}\bigr)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)\cdot\bigl(S_{2}^{-1}(\xi)\bigr)^{\dagger},

so that
S_{1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)=S_{2}^{-1}(\xi)^{\dagger}
and hence the relation among the Stokes matrix elements is
a\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)=-\overline{b(\xi)}
. We then conclude that for
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)=-\frac{1}{b(\xi)}=\frac{1}{\overline{a\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)}}=\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}}.

This proves the reality condition.
∎

Corollary 3.65
.

Using the same notation from Section
3.6.2
, we have that

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\sim_{\xi\to\infty}\overline{A\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}}.

Proof.

By the reality condition, we have that the asymptotics of
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
as
\xi\to\infty
match the asymptotics of
\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}}
as
\xi\to\infty
. But by Proposition
3.59
, we have that

\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}}\sim_{\xi\to\infty}\overline{A\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)^{-1}},

so the result follows.
∎

3.7
Non-vanishing of Stokes data

In this section, we show that given
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
with
m\neq 0
, the coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\allowbreak\theta,h,g\bigr),\xi\bigr)
is actually well defined for
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}-l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)
(i.e., that
b(\xi)\neq 0
for
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}
).

3.7.1
The case of trivial Stokes data

We begin with the following lemma dealing with the case of trivial Stokes data.

Lemma 3.66
.

Consider
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{\textnormal{fr}}
and its associated compatibly framed filtered flat bundle
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{*}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{*}^{\xi}\bigr)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Suppose that the Stokes data associated to
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{*}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{*}^{\xi}\bigr)
is trivial
(
i.e.,
S_{i}=1
for
i=1,2,3,4
and
M_{0}=1)
. Then the parameter
m
specifying the singularity of the Higgs field of
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)
must be
0
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two cases:

•

Suppose first that the parabolic weights of
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
lie in
\bigl(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\bigr)\subset\mathbb{R}
. Consider the framed wild harmonic bundle
\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0},g_{0}\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
given in Example
3.19
. Let us give a description of the associated filtered bundle
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{*}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{0},\nabla^{\xi}_{0},\tau_{*,0}^{\xi}\bigr)
. To give a description of this filtered bundle, it is enough to specify the parabolic flat bundle
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{0},\nabla^{\xi}_{0},\tau_{1/2,0}^{\xi}\bigr)
. Since
m^{(3)}=m=0
, the parabolic filtration is trivial in this case, with the holomorphic frames describing the extension of
\mathcal{E}^{\xi}
to
\mathcal{PE}_{0}^{\xi}
given by

\tau_{0}^{\xi}=g_{0}\cdot\exp\left(\left(\frac{\overline{\xi}}{2w^{2}}-\frac{\xi}{2\overline{w}^{2}}\right)H\right).

Furthermore,
g_{0}\cdot\exp\bigl(\bigl(\frac{z^{2}\overline{\xi}}{2}-\frac{\overline{z}^{2}\xi}{2}\bigr)H\bigr)
gives a global holomorphic trivialization of
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\xi}
, so that
\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\xi}\cong\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}
. In this global trivialization, the connection has the following form:

\nabla^{\xi}_{0}={\rm d}-\bigl(\xi^{-1}+\overline{\xi}\bigr)H\frac{{\rm d}w}{w^{3}}.

This connection has a frame of flat sections defined over
\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, given by
g_{0}\cdot\exp\smash{\bigl(\bigl(-\frac{z^{2}\xi^{-1}}{2}}\allowbreak-\smash{\frac{\overline{z}^{2}\xi}{2}\bigr)H\bigr)}
satisfying the appropriate asymptotics with respect to
\tau_{0}^{\xi}
(and
g_{0}
). From this fact, we see that the formal monodromy and the associated Stokes matrices are trivial.
Now going back to
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
, the fact that it comes from a harmonic bundle implies that
\operatorname{pdeg}\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}\bigr)=0
, while the assumption on the parabolic weights imply that
\operatorname{deg}\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}\bigr)=0
. Since it also has trivial Stokes data, the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for framed flat bundles presented in
[
2
]
implies that
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
is isomorphic to
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{0},\nabla^{\xi}_{0},\tau_{1/2,0}^{\xi}\bigr)
(as compatibly framed flat bundles). In particular,
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi}\bigr)
is isomorphic with
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}_{0},\nabla^{\xi}_{0}\bigr)
, so that
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\bigr)
is isomorphic to
\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0}\bigr)
. Hence,

-\bigl(z^{2}+2m\bigr){\rm d}z^{2}=\operatorname{Det}(\theta)=\operatorname{Det}(\theta_{0})=-z^{2}{\rm d}z^{2},

which implies that
m=0
.

•

The parabolic weights of
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
are both equal to
1/2
: in this case, the condition on the parabolic weights and the fact that
\operatorname{pdeg}\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}\bigr)=0
implies that
\operatorname{deg}\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi}\bigr)=1
. Hence, the exponent of formal monodromy
\Lambda(\xi)
of
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
must satisfy
\operatorname{Tr}(\Lambda(\xi))\allowbreak=-1
(see
[
4
]
), which together with the fact that the formal monodromy is trivial implies that

\Lambda(\xi)=\begin{bmatrix}n&0\\
0&-n-1\\
\end{bmatrix}

for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Now consider the degree
1
bundle
\mathcal{O}(-n)\oplus\mathcal{O}(n+1)\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
. In the
z
coordinate of
{\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{C}P^{1}}
, and the usual trivialization over that neighborhood, we consider the connection

\nabla={\rm d}+\bigl(\xi^{-1}+\overline{\xi}\bigr)Hz{\rm d}z
.
In the neighborhood
\mathbb{C}P^{1}\setminus\{0\}
with coordinate
w=\frac{1}{z}
and its usual trivialization, we have that

\nabla={\rm d}-\bigl(\xi^{-1}+\overline{\xi}\bigr)H\frac{{\rm d}w}{w^{3}}+\begin{bmatrix}n&0\\
0&-n-1\\
\end{bmatrix}\frac{{\rm d}w}{w}.

So
\nabla
is a meromorphic connection with the appropriate irregular part. The trivialization over
\mathbb{C}P^{1}\setminus\{0\}
clearly gives a compatible framing
\tau
over
z=\infty
, so we get a compatibly framed meromorphic flat bundle
(\mathcal{O}(-n)\oplus\mathcal{O}(n+1),\nabla,\tau)
.
It is easy to check that the Stokes matrices and formal monodromy associated to
(\mathcal{O}(-n)\oplus\mathcal{O}(n+1),\nabla,\tau)
are trivial. Hence, since
(\mathcal{O}(-n)\oplus\mathcal{O}(n+1),\nabla,\tau)
and
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
have the same formal type and same Stokes data, we get that
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
is isomorphic to
(\mathcal{O}(-n)\oplus\mathcal{O}({n+1}),\allowbreak\nabla,\tau)
as framed flat bundles (the proof of this fact follows part of the argument of the proof of Lemma
D.2
, for example).
On the other hand,
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
has the trivial filtration as a parabolic bundle with parabolic weights both equal to
\frac{1}{2}
, so
(\mathcal{O}(-n)\oplus\mathcal{O}(n+1),\nabla,\tau)
also gets this parabolic structure. Now notice that
\nabla
preserves the line bundles
\mathcal{O}(-n)
and
\mathcal{O}(n+1)
, and with the induced parabolic structures on the line bundles we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{pdeg}(\mathcal{O}(-n))=-n-\frac{1}{2},\qquad\operatorname{pdeg}(\mathcal{O}(n+1))=n+1-\frac{1}{2}=n+\frac{1}{2}.

This shows that no matter what
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is, we have that
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi}\bigr)
is unstable. But
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi}\bigr)
comes from a harmonic bundle, so it must also be polystable by Theorem
3.11
. This contradiction shows that
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h}_{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{1/2}^{\xi}\bigr)
cannot have parabolic weights equal to
1/2
if it has trivial monodromy data. We conclude that the only case that occurs is the previous case.

Hence, we conclude that if
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)
has trivial Stokes data, we must necessarily have
m=0
.
∎

3.7.2
Proof of the non-vanishing of Stokes data when
\boldsymbol{m\neq 0}

Recall that our Stokes data is made out of the
2\times 2
unipotent Stokes matrices
S_{i}(\xi)
for
i=1,2,3,4
and formal monodromy
M_{0}={\rm e}^{-2\pi{\rm i}\Lambda(\xi)}
that must satisfy the relation
S_{1}S_{2}S_{3}S_{4}M_{0}^{-1}=1
. We will label the off-diagonal non-trivial complex numbers of
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
,
S_{3}
and
S_{4}
by
a
,
b
,
c
and
d
, respectively, as in Section
3.4.4
. Hence,
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
is defined using
a(\xi)
and
-1/b(\xi)
using the conventions from Section
3.4.4
.

Because of the reality condition satisfied by the Stokes data, we have that for
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m}
the equality
-\overline{a\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)}=b(\xi)
holds. Hence, if we want to show that
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
is well defined, it is enough to show that
a(\xi)
does not vanish for
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
. To show this, we will need the following lemma, whose proof is easy:

Lemma 3.67
.

Let
U_{\pm}
be the set of upper
(
lower, respectively
)
unipotent
2\times 2
matrices, and
T\subset{\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
the subset of diagonal matrices. Then the set

\mathcal{M}=\bigl\{(S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4},M_{0})\in(U_{-}\times U_{+})^{2}\times T\mid S_{1}S_{2}S_{3}S_{4}M_{0}^{-1}=1\bigr\}

is a complex
2
-dimensional manifold. Furthermore, if we denote by
\mathcal{S}
the subset of
\mathcal{M}
defined by

\mathcal{S}=\{(S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},S_{4},M_{0})\in\mathcal{M}\mid S_{1}\neq S_{2}\ \text{and either}\ S_{1}=1\ \text{or}\ S_{2}=1\},

then
\mathcal{S}
is a complex
1
-dimensional submanifold with
2
components. The components are determined by whether
S_{1}=1

(
i.e.,
a=0)
or
S_{2}=1

(
i.e.,
b=0)
.

Proposition 3.68
.

Let
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{\textnormal{fr}}
with associated parameters
m^{(3)}
and
m\neq 0
. Then for
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
we have that
a\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],\xi\bigr)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let us first see what condition we get if
a(\xi)=0
for
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
. If
a(\xi)=0
, then by the relation
1+a(\xi)b(\xi)=\mu^{-1}(\xi)
from (
3.15
) we conclude that
\mu^{-1}=1
. In particular, by (
3.18
) we must have

\xi^{-1}m-m^{(3)}-\overline{m}\xi=k

for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Solving for
\xi
, we get two solutions
\xi^{\pm}_{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
given by

\xi_{k}^{\pm}=\left(\frac{-\bigl(k+m^{(3)}\bigr)\pm\sqrt{\bigl(k+m^{(3)}\bigr)^{2}+4|m|^{2}}}{2|m|^{2}}\right)m.

The values
\xi_{k}^{\pm}
are the only possible values of the twistor parameter for which we could have
a(\xi)=0

(
for our fixed
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
)
. In particular, the solutions contained in
\mathbb{H}_{m}
are given by

\xi_{k}^{+}=\left(\frac{-\bigl(k+m^{(3)}\bigr)+\sqrt{\bigl(m^{(3)}+k\bigr)^{2}+4|m|^{2}}}{2|m|^{2}}\right)m,

and from the formula we see that
\xi_{k}^{+}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, and that
\xi_{k}^{+}\to\infty
as
k\to-\infty
. Furthermore, all
\xi_{k}^{+}
are contained in the ray determined by
0
and
m
.

Because of the asymptotic behavior of
a(\xi)
as
\xi\to 0
from Section
3.6.2
, we see that
a\bigl(\xi_{k}^{+}\bigr)
cannot be
0
for all
k
, otherwise the asymptotics would not hold. Hence,
a\bigl(\xi_{k}^{+}\bigr)\neq 0
for some
k
sufficiently big. Similarly, we get from the asymptotics as
\xi\to\infty
that
a\bigl(\xi_{k}^{+}\bigr)\neq 0
for some
k
sufficiently negative.

We will now show that the fact that
a\bigl(\xi_{k}^{+}\bigr)\neq 0
for at least one
k\in\mathbb{Z}
implies that
a(\xi_{n}^{+})\neq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. To show this, we will need the following lemma:

Lemma 3.69
.

Let
\bigl(\hskip-0.3ptE,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\!\in\!\mathcal{H}^{\textnormal{fr}}
with associated parameters
m^{(3)}
and
m\neq 0
, and
consider
the curve
\xi\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{H}_{m}
given by

\xi\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)=\left(\frac{-\bigl(k+\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)+\sqrt{\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}+k\bigr)^{2}+4|m|^{2}}}{2|m|^{2}}\right)m.

Furthermore, let
\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{*}^{h}\mathcal{E}^{\xi},\nabla^{\xi},\tau_{*}^{\xi}\bigr)
denote the associated compatibly framed flat bundle of
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\allowbreak\theta,h,g\bigr)
for
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, and
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{M}
be as in Lemma
3.67
. Then we can find a continuous map
\tau\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}
such that
\tau\bigl(m^{(3)}+n\bigr)
is the Stokes data associated
\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{*}^{h}\mathcal{E}^{\xi(m^{(3)}+n)},\nabla^{\xi(m^{(3)}+n)},\tau_{*}^{\xi(m^{(3)}+n)}\bigr)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

of lemma:

Let
\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{*}^{h}\mathcal{E}^{0},\theta\bigr)
be the associated filtered Higgs bundle to
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\bigr)
, and let
(E,\theta)=\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{*}^{h}\mathcal{E}^{0},\theta\bigr)|_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}\setminus\{\infty\}}
. Near
z=\infty
, we choose a splitting of
(E,\theta)
in eigenlines, such that
\theta
has the form (
3.3
). We then have that
m^{(3)}
specifies the filtered/parabolic structure of
\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{*}^{h}\mathcal{E}^{0},\theta\bigr)
(see, for example, the construction of Lemma
3.15
). By varying
m^{(3)}
in
\mathbb{R}
, we thus vary the parabolic structure and hence the filtered Higgs bundle of parabolic degree
0
we obtain from
(E,\theta)
by following Lemma
3.15
. We will denote by
\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{*}^{h}\mathcal{E}^{0}\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr),\theta\bigr)
the filtered Higgs bundles of parabolic degree
0
that we obtain from
(E,\theta)
with parabolic structure specified by
\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Notice that the filtered/parabolic structure only depends on
\widetilde{m^{(3)}}
mod
1
, so if
h\bigl(\widetilde{m^{3}}\bigr)
denotes a curve of harmonic metrics adapted to
\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{*}^{h}\mathcal{E}^{0}\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr),\theta\bigr)
, we get by the uniqueness of harmonic metrics that for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
h\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}+n\bigr)=c(n)h\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)
for some constant
c(n)>0
.

We denote by
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)\bigr)
the corresponding curve of wild harmonic bundles, and by

\sigma\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)=\bigl[\bigl(E\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr),\overline{\partial}_{E(\widetilde{m^{(3)}})},\theta,h\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr),g\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr]

the corresponding curve of equivalence classes of framed wild harmonic bundles obtained by applying the construction of Proposition
3.23
. Notice that
g\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)
satisfies that
\smash{g\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}+n\bigr)}=\smash{g\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)\cdot\sqrt{c(n)^{-1}}}
and that

\bigl(E\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr),\overline{\partial}_{E(\widetilde{m^{(3)}})}\bigr)=\bigl(E\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}+n\bigr),\overline{\partial}_{E(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}+n)}\bigr)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence, by Example
3.20
we have that
\sigma\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)=\sigma\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}+n\bigr)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Now consider the continuous map
\tau\bigl(\widetilde{m^{3}}\bigr)\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{M}
, where
\tau\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)
is the Stokes data of the associated compatibly framed flat bundle

\bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h(\widetilde{m^{(3)}})}_{*}\mathcal{E}^{\xi(\widetilde{m^{(3)}})},\nabla^{\xi(\widetilde{m^{(3)}})},\tau_{*}^{\xi(\widetilde{m^{(3)}})}\bigr)\bigr]\qquad\text{of}\quad\sigma\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr).

By Lemma
3.66
, we have that
\tau
does not go through
(1,1,1,1,1)\in\mathcal{M}
. Hence, by the choice of
\xi\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)
we have that
\tau
lands in
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{M}
. Furthermore, since
\sigma\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}+n\bigr)=\sigma\bigl(\widetilde{m^{(3)}}\bigr)
for all
{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, we see that the same holds for
\tau
. We conclude that
\tau
satisfies the required properties.
∎

Going back to the proof of Proposition
3.68
, let
\tau
be a curve like in the previous lemma, associated to our chosen
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
. This curve must then be contained in one of the two components of
\mathcal{S}
, and it goes through all the points where
a(\xi)
could be
0
(i.e., through the points
{\tau\bigl(m^{(3)}+n\bigr)}
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
). Since the components of
\mathcal{S}
are determined by whether
a\neq 0
or
{b\neq 0}
, and we know that
a\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],\xi_{n}^{+}\bigr)=a\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],\xi\bigl(m^{(3)}+n\bigr)\bigr)\neq 0
for at least one
n
, we conclude that
a\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],\xi_{n}^{+}\bigr)\neq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since these are all the points in
\mathbb{H}_{m}
where
a\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],\xi\bigr)
could be
0
, we then have that
a\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],\xi\bigr)\neq 0
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
.
∎

By the remark at the beginning of the section, we then conclude.

Corollary 3.70
.

Given
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
with parameter
m\neq 0
, the magnetic twistor coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{m}\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],\xi\bigr)
is well defined for
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}-l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)
and it takes values in
\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

4
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}}
and the Ooguri–Vafa space

In this section, we show that there is a natural one-to-one correspondence between a subset of
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
and the Ooguri–Vafa space
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}
. The correspondence is such that the coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
for
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
built on the last section match the twistor coordinates
\mathcal{X}_{e}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
of
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}
.

4.1
Matching parameters with the Ooguri–Vafa space

We start by comparing the electric twistor coordinates

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{e}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)=\exp\bigl[\pi\xi^{-1}z+{\rm i}\theta_{e}+\pi\xi\overline{z}\bigr],

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)=\exp\bigl[-2\pi{\rm i}\bigl(\xi^{-1}m-m^{(3)}-\overline{m}\xi\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\phantom{\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)}{}=\exp\bigl[\pi\xi^{-1}(-2{\rm i}m)+{\rm i}\bigl(2\pi m^{(3)}\bigr)+\pi\xi\overline{(-2{\rm i}m)}\bigr].

(4.1)

The above formulas suggest that
z=-2{\rm i}m
, while
\theta_{e}=2\pi m^{(3)}
(mod
2\pi
).

To find the analog of the magnetic angle, we will use the asymptotics of
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
from Proposition
3.59

\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\sim_{\xi\to 0}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\xi}m\operatorname{Log}_{p}\left(-\frac{m}{2e}\right)+{\rm i}m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}_{p}(-m)+{\rm i}\pi+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\right),

which we can rewrite as

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\sim_{\xi\to 0}

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{{\rm i}}{2\xi}\left((-2{\rm i}m)\operatorname{Log}_{p}\left(-\frac{2{\rm i}m}{4{\rm i}}\right)-(-2{\rm i}m)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.{}+{\rm i}m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}_{p}(-m)+{\rm i}\pi+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\right).

(4.2)

Comparing the last expression with the asymptotics of
\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
from Proposition
2.2

\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)\sim_{\xi\to 0}\exp\biggl(-\frac{{\rm i}}{2\xi}\left(z\operatorname{Log}_{p}\left(\frac{z}{\Lambda}\right)-z\right)+{\rm i}\theta_{m}+\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\sum_{s\neq 0,s\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{s}{\rm e}^{{\rm i}s\theta_{e}}K_{0}(2\pi|sz|)\biggr)

(4.3)

we see that if
z=-2{\rm i}m
, the
\xi^{-1}
term matches if we pick
\Lambda=4{\rm i}
. On the other hand, when comparing the
\xi^{0}
term, we see that by matching the imaginary part (notice that the term with the sum of Bessel functions is real), we get that
\theta_{m}
should correspond to

m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}_{p}(-m)+\pi+\operatorname{Im}\bigg(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\bigg)\quad(\text{mod}2\pi).

4.1.1
The magnetic angle on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}}

The matching of parameters from above will let us define an analog of the Ooguri–Vafa magnetic angle on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
. Before defining it, we will need the following lemmas.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose that
\bigl(E_{i},\overline{\partial}_{E_{i}},\theta_{i},h_{i},g_{i}\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{\textnormal{fr}}
for
i=1,2
have parameters
m_{i}
and
m^{(3)}_{i}
describing the singularity of
\theta_{i}
and
\overline{\partial}_{E_{i}}
at
z=\infty

(
as in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
)
)
. If there is an isomorphism between these two elements, then
m_{1}=m_{2}
and
m^{(3)}_{1}=m^{(3)}_{2}
. In particular, it makes sense to associate
m
and
m^{(3)}
to elements of
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}
.

Proof.

If the two elements of
\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
are isomorphic, then

-\bigl(z^{2}+2m_{1}\bigr){\rm d}z^{2}=\operatorname{Det}(\theta_{1})=\operatorname{Det}(\theta_{2})=-\bigl(z^{2}+2m_{2}\bigr){\rm d}z^{2},

so that
m_{1}=m_{2}
. On the other hand, if we denote the compatible frames (and their extensions to local frames around
z=\infty
) by
g_{1}=(e_{1},e_{2})
,
g_{2}=(f_{1},f_{2})
, and the isomorphism by
T
, we then have

m^{(3)}_{2}f_{1}=\overline{\partial}_{E_{2}}(-2\overline{w}\partial_{\overline{w}})(f_{1})|_{w=0}=T\bigl(\overline{\partial}_{E_{1}}(-2\overline{w}\partial_{\overline{w}})(e_{1})|_{w=0}\bigr)=T\bigl(m^{(3)}_{1}e_{1}\bigr)=m^{(3)}_{1}f_{1},

so that
m^{(3)}_{1}=m^{(3)}_{2}
.
∎

Definition 4.2
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{C}
and
m^{(3)}\in\bigl(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\bigr]
. We will denote by
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}
the set of equivalence classes whose singularity with respect to the compatible framing is described by
m
and
m^{(3)}
.

For the following proposition, we will need the next lemma:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{C}
and
m^{(3)}\in\bigl(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\bigr]\subset\mathbb{R}
. Then up to equivalence, there is a unique polystable filtered Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E}_{*},\theta)
with
\operatorname{Tr}(\theta)=0
,
\operatorname{Det}(\theta)=-\bigl(z^{2}+2m\bigr){\rm d}z^{2}
, and parabolic weights determined by
m^{(3)}
as follows:

•

if
m^{(3)}\in\bigl(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\bigr)
, then for the eigenline decomposition near
\infty
of the induced
\frac{1}{2}
-parabolic Higgs bundle
(\mathcal{E}_{1/2},\theta)
, we have that
\pm m^{(3)}
is the weight associated to the line corresponding to the eigenvalue
\pm\bigl(z+\frac{m}{z}+\cdots\bigr){\rm d}z
.

•

if
m^{(3)}=\frac{1}{2}
then the parabolic structure of the induced
\frac{1}{2}
-parabolic structure
(\mathcal{E}_{1/2},\theta)
is the trivial filtration with weight
\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

In Appendix
D
.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

We have a
{\rm U}(1)
-action on
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}
given by

{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]=\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta/2}\cdot g\bigr)\bigr],

(4.4)

where if
g=(e_{1},e_{2})
, then
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta/2}\cdot(e_{1},e_{2})=\bigl({\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta/2}e_{1},{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\theta/2}e_{2}\bigr)
. Furthermore, for
m\neq 0
we have that
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
is a
{\rm U}(1)
-torsor under this action.

Proof.

First notice that
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
is isomorphic to
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\pi}\cdot g\bigr)
by the isomorphism
-\text{Id}_{E}
(where
\text{Id}_{E}\colon E\to E
denotes the identity map), so that the map
{\rm U}(1)\times\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}\to\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
given by

{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)=\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta/2}\cdot g\bigr)\bigr]

is well defined.

On the other hand, if
T
is an isomorphism between
\bigl(E_{1},\overline{\partial}_{E_{1}},\theta_{1},h_{1},g_{1}\bigr)
and
\bigl(E_{2},\overline{\partial}_{E_{2}},\theta_{2},\allowbreak h_{2},g_{2}\bigr)
, then clearly
T
also gives an isomorphism between
\bigl(E_{1},\overline{\partial}_{E_{1}},\theta_{1},h_{1},{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta/2}\cdot g_{1}\bigr)
and
\smash{\bigl(E_{2},\overline{\partial}_{E_{2}}},\allowbreak\theta_{2},\smash{h_{2},{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta/2}\cdot g_{2}\bigr)}
. Hence we get an
{\rm U}(1)
-action
{\rm U}(1)\times\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\to\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
, defined by (
4.4
).

Now let
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)\subset\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
be as in Definition
4.2
. Clearly, the
{\rm U}(1)
-action on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
restricts to an action on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
. Let us now check that this action is freely transitive:

•

The action is free. Let
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\neq 1
. From the way Stokes data transforms under changes of compatible framing, it is easy to check that

{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)=\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\bigl({\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr),

(4.5)

i.e.,
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
is equivariant with respect to the
{\rm U}(1)
-action on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
and the natural
{\rm U}(1)
-action on
\mathbb{C}
. On the other hand, since
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
is valued in
\mathbb{C}^{*}
, equation (
4.5
) and the fact that
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\neq 1
implies that
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)\neq\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\bigl({\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)
. Since Stokes data is an isomorphism invariant, we must have
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\neq{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]
.

•

The action is transitive: Let
\bigl[\bigl(E_{i},\overline{\partial}_{E_{i}},\theta_{i},h_{i},g_{i}\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
for
i=1,2
. By Lemma
4.3
, we have that the underlying filtered Higgs bundles
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{h_{i}}_{*}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{0},\theta_{i}\bigr)
are isomorphic. But then we must have that
\bigl(E_{1},\overline{\partial}_{E_{1}},\theta_{1},h_{1}\bigr)
is isomorphic to
\bigl(E_{2},\overline{\partial}_{E_{2}},\theta_{2},h_{2}\bigr)
. Following the construction of Proposition
3.23
, we then find an isomorphism between
\bigl(E_{1},\overline{\partial_{1}},\theta_{1},h_{1},g_{1}\bigr)
and
\bigl(E_{2},\overline{\partial_{2}},\theta_{2},h_{2},\widetilde{g}_{2}\bigr)
for some compatible frame
\widetilde{g}_{2}
. Now
g_{2}
and
\widetilde{g_{2}}
must be
{\rm SU}(2)
eigenframes of
\theta_{2}\bigl(w^{3}\partial_{w}\bigr)|_{w=0}
with the corresponding fixed order of the eigenvalues given by the form of the singularity, so we must have
g_{2}={\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\widetilde{g}_{2}
for some
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\in{\rm U}(1)
. Hence,

{\rm e}^{2{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E_{1},\overline{\partial_{1}},\theta_{1},h_{1},g_{1}\bigr)\bigr]={\rm e}^{2{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E_{2},\overline{\partial_{2}},\theta_{2},h_{2},\widetilde{g}_{2}\bigr)\bigr]=\bigl[\bigl(E_{2},\overline{\partial_{2}},\theta_{2},h_{2},g_{2}\bigr)\bigr].

∎

Recall that for
m\neq 0
we have the correspondence

\theta_{m}\iff m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}_{p}(-m)+\pi+\operatorname{Im}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\right)\quad(\text{mod}2\pi).

(4.6)

Because of the equivariance of
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
under the
{\rm U}(1)
-actions on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
and
\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we see that acting on
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
by
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}
shifts the quantity to the right of equation (
4.6
) by
\theta
. This motivates the following definitions:

Definition 4.5
.

Let
m\neq 0
. The marked point of the
{\rm U}(1)
-torsor
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
is the unique element
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
such that

\exp\left({\rm i}m^{(3)}\operatorname{Arg}_{p}(-m)+{\rm i}\pi+{\rm i}\operatorname{Im}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\right)\right)=1.

Definition 4.6
.

For
m\neq 0
, the magnetic angle
\theta_{m}
of
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
is defined to be the unique real number mod
2\pi
such that

\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]={\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta_{m}}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]
,
where
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]
is the marked point of
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
.

Notice that our definition of marked point uses a branch of the
\operatorname{Arg}
function (with
\operatorname{Arg}(z)\in[-\pi,\pi)
), so the magnetic angle is not a priori a global continuous function of
m
. In the following, we will compute how the magnetic angle jumps when we go around a loop in the
m
parameter. We will see that it will match the jump of the magnetic angle of the Ooguri–Vafa space.

Definition 4.7
.

We will say that a map
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{H}^{\textnormal{fr}}
is a continuous path, if the elements
{\gamma(t):=\bigl(E_{t},\overline{\partial}_{E_{t}},\theta_{t},h_{t},g_{t}\bigr)}
satisfy:

•

The vector bundles
E_{t}\to\mathbb{C}P^{1}
fit into a continuous vector bundle
E\to[0,1]\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, with
E|_{\{t\}\times\mathbb{C}P^{1}}=E_{t}
, and with trivializations having transition functions depending smoothly on the points of
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
and continuously on
t\in[0,1]
.

•

In the above trivializations, the structures
\overline{\partial}_{E_{t}}
,
\theta_{t}
and
h_{t}
vary continuously in
t
.

•

The elements of the frames
g_{t}
give a continuous section of
E|_{[0,1]\times\{\infty\}}
.

Furthermore, we will say that a map
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}
is a continuous path if there is a lift to a continuous path
\widetilde{\gamma}\colon[0,1]\to\mathcal{H}^{\textnormal{fr}}
.

Remark 4.8
.

A way to construct such paths is, for example, by employing
[
28
, Proposition 4.9]
and the constructions of Lemma
3.15
and Proposition
3.23
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
l:=\{m\in\mathbb{C}\mid m\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\}
, and let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}
be a loop in
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}
such that the associated curve
m(\gamma(t))
of
m
parameters of
\gamma(t)
gives a counterclockwise loop around
{m=0}
with
m(\gamma(0))\in l
. Then

\lim_{t\to 1}\theta_{m}(\gamma(t))=\theta_{m}(\gamma(0))+2\pi m^{(3)}-\pi.

Proof.

We will use our usual notations for Stokes data as in Section
3.4.4
.

The magnetic angle is defined in terms of the
\xi^{0}
term of the asymptotics of the Stokes data
{a(\gamma(t),\xi)}
as
\xi\to 0
along
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m(\gamma(t))}
, or by the
\xi^{0}
term of the asymptotics of the Stokes data
-1/b(\gamma(t),\xi)
as
\xi\to 0
along
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{-m(\gamma(t))}
. We will look at the monodromy of
a(\gamma(t),\xi)
, but a similar argument holds if we use
-1/b(\gamma(t),\xi)
.

As in Section
3.4.4
, we denote by
\Phi_{i}
the frame of flat sections of
\nabla^{\xi}
defined on the extended sector
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i}(\xi)
. As we move around the loop
\gamma(t)
, the labelings of the sectors move in a clockwise manner (recall the conventions of the
m
dependence of the labelings of Section
3.4.4
). After going around the loop, we get that the labelings moved in such a way that the following sectors are interchanged:
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{1}(\xi)\iff\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{3}(\xi)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{2}(\xi)\iff\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{4}(\xi)
. If we denote by
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}
the frame of flat sections obtained after going around the loop, it is easy to see that we get the following relations:

\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}=\Phi_{3}\cdot M_{0}^{-1},\qquad\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}=\Phi_{4}\cdot M_{0}^{-1},\qquad\widetilde{\Phi}_{3}=\Phi_{1},\qquad\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}=\Phi_{2}.

(4.7)

Now recall the relations that we have among the entries of the Stokes matrices (
3.15
) and among the flat sections (
3.16
). Using these relations and (
4.7
), we find that

\widetilde{a}=\frac{\widetilde{s}_{3}\wedge\widetilde{s}_{1}}{\widetilde{s}_{2}\wedge\widetilde{s}_{1}}=\mu^{-1}\frac{s_{1}\wedge s_{3}}{s_{4}\wedge s_{3}}=\mu^{-2}c=-\mu^{-1}a.

Since
-\mu^{-1}(\gamma(0),\xi)=\exp\bigl(\pi\xi^{-1}(-2{\rm i}m)+{\rm i}\bigl(2\pi m^{(3)}-\pi\bigr)+\pi\xi\overline{(-2{\rm i}m)}\bigr)
, from the asymptotics of
-\mu^{-1}(\gamma(0),\xi)a(\gamma(0),\xi)
as
\xi\to 0
along
\mathbb{H}_{m}
we conclude what we want.
∎

Hence, we see that the magnetic angle has the same monodromy as the usual Ooguri–Vafa magnetic angle.

4.2
Matching the twistor coordinates

By taking
z=-2{\rm i}m
and
\theta_{e}=2\pi m^{(3)}
, we clearly have that for
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
, the following holds (recall (
4.1
))

\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)=\mathcal{X}_{e}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)(z,\theta_{e})
.
So the remaining question is whether the magnetic twistor coordinate
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
on
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
matches
\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
, under the appropriate matching of parameters.

Theorem 4.10
.

Fix
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
and let
\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\textnormal{ov}}(\xi)
be the magnetic twistor coordinate of the Ooguri–Vafa space with
\Lambda=4{\rm i}
. Then by taking
z=-2{\rm i}m
,
\theta_{e}=2\pi m^{(3)}
and
\theta_{m}=\theta_{m}\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)
, we have that
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)=\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\textnormal{ov}}(\xi)(z,\theta_{e},\theta_{m})
for all
{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}-l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)}
.

Proof.

We will abbreviate the notation and just write
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
.
We consider the quotient
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)/\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
as a function of
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}-l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)
. Because both functions have the same jumping behavior along
l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)
, we have that
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)/\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
extends to a continuous function on
\mathbb{C}^{*}
that is holomorphic in
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}-l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)
. Furthermore, because of our matching of parameters and the asymptotics (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) of both functions, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\to 0}\frac{\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)}{\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)}=r,

(4.8)

where

\displaystyle r=\exp\biggl(\operatorname{Re}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma^{*}A_{11}\right)-\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\sum_{s\neq 0,s\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{s}{\rm e}^{{\rm i}s\theta_{e}}K_{0}(2\pi|sz|)\biggr)\in\mathbb{R}.

The fact that
r
is real can be easily checked by noticing that
K_{0}(2\pi|sz|)\in\mathbb{R}
and that the sum is over
s\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, which is invariant under
s\to-s
.

Using the reality condition of both coordinates, we also get that

\lim_{\xi\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)}{\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)}=\lim_{\xi\to\infty}\frac{\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}^{-1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)}}{\overline{\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}-1}\bigl(-1/\overline{\xi}\bigr)}}=r^{-1}.

Hence, we can extend
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)/\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
to a continuous function on
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
which is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}^{*}-l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)
. An application of Morera’s theorem then shows that
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)/\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
is a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{C}P^{1}
, and hence constant. In particular, we conclude that

\frac{\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)}{\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)}=r=r^{-1}
,
so that
r=\pm 1
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)=\pm\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
. To fix the sign, notice that
r
is an exponential of a real number (recall equation (
4.8
)), so that we conclude that
r=1
and then
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)=\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
for all
{\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}-l_{\pm}(-2{\rm i}m)}
.
∎

4.3
The Hyperkähler structure on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}}

Consider the Ooguri–Vafa space
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}
together with its torus fibration
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}\to\mathcal{B}
. Throughout this section, we fix the cut-off parameter specifying
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}
to be
\Lambda=4{\rm i}
.

Definition 4.11
.

We denote

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}(\mathcal{B}):=\bigl\{\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\!\in\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\mid\operatorname{Det}(\theta)=-\bigl(z^{2}+2m\bigr){\rm d}z^{2}\ \text{with $-2{\rm i}m\in\mathcal{B}$}\bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}_{*}(\mathcal{B}):=\bigl\{\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\!\in\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\mid\operatorname{Det}(\theta)=-\bigl(z^{2}+2m\bigr){\rm d}z^{2}\ \text{with $m\neq 0$ and $-2{\rm i}m\in\mathcal{B}$}\bigr\}.

Here
z\in\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{C}P^{1}
denotes the fixed holomorphic coordinate from Section
3.3
. Furthermore, we denote
\mathcal{B}_{*}=\mathcal{B}\setminus\{0\}
and
\mathcal{M}_{*}^{\textnormal{ov}}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\textnormal{ov}}
the points of the Ooguri–Vafa space over
\mathcal{B}_{*}
.

The results of Sections
4.1
and
4.2
then allow us to show the following.

Theorem 4.12
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}_{*}(\mathcal{B})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\textnormal{ov}}_{*}
such that
{\mathcal{X}_{e}=\mathcal{X}_{e}^{\text{ov}}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}=\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}
. Under this correspondence
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}_{*}(\mathcal{B})
acquires the structure of a hyperkähler manifold. For
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, the twistor family
\Omega(\xi)
of holomorphic symplectic forms on
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}_{*}(\mathcal{B})
is given by

\Omega(\xi)=\frac{{\rm d}\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)}{\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)}\wedge\frac{{\rm d}\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)}{\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)}.

(4.9)

Proof.

Consider the open cover of
\mathcal{B}_{*}\times{\rm U}(1)
given by (compare with the end of Section
2.2
)

\displaystyle U_{1}:=\bigl\{\bigl(z,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}x^{3}}\bigr)\in\mathcal{B}_{*}\times{\rm U}(1)\mid{\rm e}^{z/4{\rm i}}\not\in\mathbb{R}_{<0}\bigr\},

\displaystyle U_{2}:=\bigl\{\bigl(z,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}x^{3}}\bigr)\in\mathcal{B}_{*}\times{\rm U}(1)\mid{\rm e}^{z/4{\rm i}}\not\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\bigr\}.

We then can write
U_{1}\cap U_{2}=V_{1}\cup V_{2}
, where

\displaystyle V_{1}=\bigl\{\bigl(z,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}x^{3}}\bigr)\in\mathcal{B}_{*}\times{\rm U}(1)\mid\operatorname{Im}\bigl({\rm e}^{z/4{\rm i}}\bigr)>0\bigr\},

\displaystyle U_{-}=\bigl\{\bigl(z,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}x^{3}}\bigr)\in\mathcal{B}_{*}\times{\rm U}(1)\mid\operatorname{Im}\bigl({\rm e}^{z/4{\rm i}}\bigr)<0\bigr\}.

Furthermore, let
\pi\colon\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{B}_{*}\times S^{1}
be defined by
\pi\bigl(\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)=\bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}}\bigr)
, where
m
and
m^{(3)}
are the associated parameters of
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]
specifying the singularity. We then have that
\bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}},{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta_{m}}\bigr)
, where
\theta_{m}
is the magnetic angle from Definition
4.6
, gives coordinates for
\pi^{-1}\bigl(U_{1}\times S^{1}\bigr)\subset\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})
. Similarly, Proposition
4.9
implies that we can find another magnetic angle coordinate
\widetilde{\theta}_{m}
over
U_{2}\times S^{1}
such that
\bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}},{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\widetilde{\theta}_{m}}\bigr)
gives coordinates for
\pi^{-1}\bigl(U_{2}\times S^{1}\bigr)
, and such that the change of coordinates map
\phi\colon(V_{1}\cup V_{2})\times{\rm U}(1)\to(V_{1}\cup V_{2})\times{\rm U}(1)
is given by

\phi\bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}},{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta_{m}}\bigr)=\begin{cases}\bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}},{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta_{m}}\bigr)&\text{if}\ \bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}}\bigr)\in V_{1},\\
\bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}},{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta_{m}+2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}-{\rm i}\pi}\bigr)&\text{if}\ \bigl(-2{\rm i}m,{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}}\bigr)\in V_{2}.\end{cases}

This matches the description of
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}_{*}
given at the end of Section
2.2
, and hence gives a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}_{*}(\mathcal{B})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\textnormal{ov}}_{*}
such that
\mathcal{X}_{e}=\mathcal{X}_{e}^{\text{ov}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}=\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}
. The identification induces a hyperkähler structure on
\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})
, and by Theorem
4.10
and (
2.8
), the twistor family of holomorphic symplectic forms is given by the formula (
4.9
).
∎

However, this is not the end of the story, since the hyperkähler structure of
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}_{*}
actually extends to the points over
0\in\mathcal{B}
(see, for example,
[
16
]
or
[
13
, Section 4.1]
). The fiber over
0\in\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}\to\mathcal{B}
is a torus with a node (recall Figure
1
). In the following, we show that under our identification of parameters, we get the same picture for the elements of
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
over
m=0
. We remark that while
\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)
clearly extends to the elements of
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})
with
m=0
(recall (
4.1
)), it is not clear whether
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)
does, since the condition
m\neq 0
is heavily used in the definition and its properties. This issue already appears for
\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
, since (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) are ill-defined over
{0\in\mathcal{B}}
.

4.3.1
The central fiber of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}}

Recall that given
m\in\mathbb{C}
and
m^{(3)}\in\bigl(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\bigr]
, we denote by
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
the subset of equivalence classes of
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}
with corresponding parameters
m
and
m^{(3)}
describing the singularities. We have only shown that
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(m,m^{(3)}\bigr)
is not empty when
m\neq 0
(see Lemma
3.15
and Proposition
3.23
). In Appendix
E
, we show that
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(0,m^{(3)}\bigr)
is also not empty for every
m^{(3)}\in\bigl(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\bigr]
.

We now prove the following Lemma, which is analogous to Proposition
4.4
.

Lemma 4.13
.

For
m^{(3)}\neq 0
, we have that
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}\bigl(0,m^{(3)}\bigr)
is a
{\rm U}(1)
-torsor under the
{\rm U}(1)
-action defined on Proposition
4.4
.

Proof.

Let
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}\bigl(0,m^{(3)}\bigr)
for
m^{(3)}\neq 0
. Let us now show that the
{\rm U}(1)
-action is freely transitive:

•

The action is free: by the taking the associated Stokes data, we see that the formal monodromy of the flat connection
\nabla^{\xi}
turns out to be
{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}}\neq 1
(notice that it does not depend on
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
). Using the notation from Section
3.4.1
, we then see that the relation
1+a(\xi)b(\xi)=\mu^{-1}(\xi)={\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}m^{(3)}}
implies that
a(\xi)\neq 0
and
b(\xi)\neq 0
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. On the other hand, let
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\neq 1
. It is then easy to check that

a\bigl(\xi,{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)={\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}a\bigl(\xi,\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr),

and since
a(\xi)\neq 0
, we must have that

a\bigl(\xi,{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr)\neq a\bigl(\xi,\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\bigr).

Since Stokes data is an isomorphism invariant, we conclude that

\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\neq{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr],

so the action is free.

•

The action is transitive: the same proof as Proposition
4.4
.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}(0,0)
is just a point.

Proof.

Let
\bigl[\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0},g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}(0,0)
be the framed wild harmonic bundle from Example
3.19
, and let
\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]\in\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}(0,0)
be any other framed wild harmonic bundle. By the same proof of the transitivity of the
{\rm U}(1)
-action of Proposition
4.4
, we have that

\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]={\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0},g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]

for some
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\in{\rm U}(1)
.

But from the description of
\bigl[\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0},g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]
in Example
3.19
it is easy to see that
\bigl[\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0},g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]={\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\cdot\bigl[\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0},g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]
for every
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}\in{\rm U}(1)
, the isomorphism being the map
T\colon E_{0}\to E_{0}
described in the global canonical frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
by

T=\begin{bmatrix}{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\frac{\theta}{2}}&0\\
0&{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\frac{\theta}{2}}\end{bmatrix}.

Hence,
\bigl[\bigl(E_{0},\overline{\partial}_{E_{0}},\theta_{0},h_{0},g_{0}\bigr)\bigr]=\bigl[\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\bigr]
and
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}(0,0)
is just a point.
∎

Corollary 4.15
.

The hyperkähler structure of
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}_{*}(\mathcal{B})
extends to
\mathfrak{X}^{\textnormal{fr}}(\mathcal{B})
.

Proof.

In Theorem
4.12
, we established a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}_{*}(\mathcal{B})
and
\mathcal{M}_{*}^{\text{ov}}
such that
\mathcal{X}_{e}(\xi)=\mathcal{X}_{e}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}(\xi)=\mathcal{X}_{m}^{\text{ov}}(\xi)
, and
z=-2{\rm i}m
,
\theta_{e}=2\pi m^{(3)}
. On the other hand, we know that the fiber of
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}\to\mathcal{B}
over
0\in\mathcal{B}
is a singular torus with a node (see Figure
1
) and that the node corresponds to
z=\theta_{e}=0
. Lemmas
4.13
and
4.14
then imply that our one-to-one correspondence
\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})\cong\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}_{*}
naturally extends to
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})\cong\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}
. Since the hyperkähler structure of
\mathcal{M}_{*}^{\text{ov}}
extends over the singular fiber
[
16
]
to
\mathcal{M}^{\text{ov}}
, and the hyperkähler structure on
\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})
is induced from the one-to-one correspondence of Theorem
4.12
, we conclude that the hyperkähler structure of
\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})
extends to
\mathfrak{X}^{{\rm fr}}(\mathcal{B})
.
∎

Overall, joining Theorems
4.10
and
4.12
, and Corollary
4.15
, we obtain our main result stated at the beginning in Theorem
1.1
.

Appendix A
Estimates for the connection form

We use the setting and notation of the beginning of the proof of Proposition
3.23
. We want to prove the following.

Lemma A.1
.

In the orthonormal frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
, the Chern connection is expressed as

D={\rm d}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}a(v_{1})&0\\
0&a(v_{2})\\
\end{bmatrix}\left(\frac{{\rm d}w}{w}-\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}}\right)+\text{regular terms at $w=0$}.

Proof.

The gauge transformation
g
that satisfies
(v_{1},v_{2})\cdot g=(e_{1},e_{2})
is given by

g=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{|v_{1}|_{h}}&-\frac{h(v_{1},v_{2})}{|v_{1}|^{2}_{h}|v_{2}-h(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}|_{h}^{-2}v_{1}|_{h}}\\
0&\frac{1}{|v_{2}-h(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}|_{h}^{-2}v_{1}|_{h}}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Furthermore, we know that
|v_{i}|_{h}^{2}=|w|^{-2a(v_{i})}f_{i}(w)
, where
f_{i}(w)
is a positive real function that is bounded near
w=0
(this is a consequence of Theorem
3.22
). If
A
denotes the connection matrix of
D
in the frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
, then we have that

A=g^{-1}{\rm d}g+g^{-1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-a(v_{1})&0\\
0&-a(v_{2})\\
\end{bmatrix}\frac{{\rm d}w}{w}+\text{regular}\right)g.

By using the fact that the off-diagonal terms of
g
and the off-diagonal terms of the regular terms of the connection matrix of
D
in the frame
(v_{1},v_{2})
go to 0 exponentially as
w\to 0
, it is easy to check that

g^{-1}\left(\begin{bmatrix}-a(v_{1})&0\\
0&-a(v_{2})\\
\end{bmatrix}\frac{{\rm d}w}{w}+\text{regular}\right)g=\begin{bmatrix}-a(v_{1})&0\\
0&-a(v_{2})\\
\end{bmatrix}\frac{{\rm d}w}{w}+\text{regular}.

On the other hand, the fact that in the frame
(v_{1},v_{2})
we have that

D=D_{0}+\text{regular}={\rm d}+H^{-1}\partial H,

where
H
is the matrix of the hermitian metric in the frame
(v_{1},v_{2})
,
implies that the functions
{f_{i}^{-1}\partial_{z}f_{i}}
are regular at
w=0
. Since the
f_{i}
are real, we get that
f_{i}^{-1}\partial_{\overline{z}}f_{i}
is also regular, so that
f_{i}^{-1}{\rm d}f_{i}=-f_{i}{\rm d}f_{i}^{-1}
is regular at
w=0
. From this fact, we conclude that

g^{-1}dg=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}a(v_{1})&0\\
0&a(v_{2})\\
\end{bmatrix}\left(\frac{{\rm d}w}{w}+\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}}\right)+\text{regular}.

Hence, in the frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
, we get that

D={\rm d}+A={\rm d}-\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}a(v_{1})&0\\
0&a(v_{2})\\
\end{bmatrix}\left(\frac{{\rm d}w}{w}-\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}}\right)+\text{regular}.

∎

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
3.53

The goal of this appendix is to show that the asymptotics of Lemma
3.37
hold uniformly in
{\xi\in U(\xi_{0})}
, where
\xi_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
U(\xi_{0})
is some small bounded neighborhood of
\xi_{0}
. Because of the expression of the flat frames
\Phi_{i}(\xi)
in terms of extensions of the compatible frames
g=(e_{1},e_{2})
of the harmonic bundles, it is easy to check that it is enough to show that

•

\Sigma_{i}\bigl(\widehat{F}(\xi)\bigr)\to 1
uniformly in
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
as
w\to 0
with
w\in S_{i}\subset\widehat{\operatorname{Sect}}_{i}(\xi)
. Here
S_{i}
is the sector defined at the beginning of Section
3.5.3
.

•

g_{\xi}(w)\to 1
uniformly in
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
as
w\to 0
.

We will only prove the second statement, since the first one follows from the proof of
[
5
, Theorem 7]
. For the proof of the second statement, we will follow similar arguments and notations as those found in
[
2
]
, where they construct
g_{\xi}(w)
for
\xi=1
.

We will denote by
D\subset\mathbb{C}P^{1}
the unit disc centered at
w=\frac{1}{z}=0
with radial coordinate
r
. We also denote by
E
the vector bundle corresponding to an element
\bigl(E,\overline{\partial}_{E},\theta,h,g\bigr)\in\mathcal{H}^{{\rm fr}}
, trivialized over
D
by an extension of the compatible framing at
w=0
to a local
{\rm SU}(2)
framing. Finally, we denote by
\Gamma(D,\operatorname{End}(E))
the set of sections (with no regularity assumed) of
{\operatorname{End}(E)\to D}
. For
\delta>0
, we then define the weighted Sobolev spaces, as in
[
2
]

\displaystyle L_{\delta}^{p}=\left\{f\in\Gamma(D,\operatorname{End}(E))\,\Big|\,\frac{f}{r^{\delta+2/p}}\in L^{p}(D,\operatorname{End}(E))\right\},

\displaystyle L_{\delta}^{p,k}=\left\{f\in\Gamma(D,\operatorname{End}(E))\,\Big|\,\frac{D\bigl(\overline{\partial}_{E},h\bigr)^{j}f_{i}}{r^{i(k-j)}}\in L^{p}_{\delta}\ \text{for}\ i=0,3,\,0\leq j\leq k\right\},

where derivatives are considered in the weak sense, and where
f_{0}
and
f_{3}
denote the diagonal and off-diagonal components of
f\in\operatorname{End}(E)
. The reason for the strange indexing notation for the diagonal and off-diagonal part is so that our notation agrees with
[
2
]
. The highest order pole of the singularity (cubic order in our case) acts non-trivially via the adjoint action on the off-diagonal part of a section
f\in\Gamma(D,\operatorname{End}(E))
(hence the “
3
” subscript), while the whole singular part acts trivially on the diagonal part of
f
(hence the “
0
” subscript).

Similarly, we have the Banach spaces
C^{k}_{\delta}
defined by

C^{k}_{\delta}=\left\{f\in\Gamma\bigl(\overline{D},\text{End(E)}\bigr)\,\Big|\,\frac{f}{r^{\delta}}\in C^{k}\bigl(\overline{D},\operatorname{End}(E)\bigr)\right\}.

The gauge transformation
g_{\xi}
is built as a solution to the following problem: with respect to an extension of the compatible framing, we have the expression

\bigl(\nabla^{\xi}\bigr)^{0,1}=\overline{\partial}-\xi H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}^{3}}-\left(\xi\overline{m}+\frac{m^{(3)}}{2}\right)H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}}+a_{\text{reg}}^{0,1}+\xi\theta_{\text{reg}}^{\dagger_{h}},

where
a_{\text{reg}}^{0,1}
and
\theta_{\text{reg}}^{\dagger_{h}}
denote the regular parts of
D\bigl(\overline{\partial}_{E},h\bigr)^{(0,1)}
and
\theta^{\dagger_{h}}
, respectively. The gauge transformation
g_{\xi}
then satisfies

\displaystyle g_{\xi}\cdot\bigl(\nabla^{\xi}\bigr)^{0,1}=\overline{\partial}-\xi H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}^{3}}-\left(\xi\overline{m}+\frac{m^{(3)}}{2}\right)H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}},\qquad g_{\xi}(w=0)=1,

\displaystyle g_{\xi,0}-1\in C^{0}_{\delta}g_{\xi,3}\in C^{0}_{2+\delta}\qquad\text{for some}\quad\delta>0.

(B.1)

In order to show the existence of such a
g_{\xi}
, we slightly extend some of the results in
[
2
, Section 7]
in order to get statements for families in
\xi
.

Lemma B.1
.

Take
\delta\in\mathbb{R}-\mathbb{Z}
and
p>2
. On the unit disk, the problem

\frac{\partial f}{\partial\overline{w}}=g

(B.2)

has a solution
f=T(g)
such that
|f|_{C^{0}_{-1+\delta}}\leq c|g|_{L^{p}_{-2+\delta}}
. Furthermore, if
\lambda(\xi)
is a continuous function of
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
, then the same is true if
\delta-\operatorname{Re}(\lambda(\xi))\in\mathbb{R}-\mathbb{Z}
for the problem

\frac{\partial f}{\partial\overline{w}}-\frac{\lambda(\xi)}{2\overline{w}}f=g.

(B.3)

By picking
U(\xi_{0})
small enough, we have
|T_{\xi}(g)|_{C^{0}_{-1+\delta}}\leq c|g|_{L^{p}_{-2+\delta}}
for all
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
, for a uniform constant
c
.

Proof.

We will only show the last two statements of the lemma, since the first is the same as
[
2
, Lemma 7.2]
.

By the same argument given in
[
2
, Lemma 7.2]
, we can assume for our problem that
\delta-\operatorname{Re}(\lambda(\xi))\in(0,1)
for
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
. By shrinking
U(\xi_{0})
further if necessary, we can assume that
{\delta-\operatorname{Re}(\lambda(\xi))\in(\delta_{0},\delta_{1})}
for
0<\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}<1
and
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
. If
T
denotes the solution operator for the first inhomogeneous Cauchy–Riemann problem (
B.2
), then
T_{\xi}(g):=r^{\lambda(\xi)}T\bigl(r^{-\lambda(\xi)}g\bigr)
is the solution operator for (
B.3
).

Now notice that by applying Hölder’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle|T_{\xi}(g)(w)|

\displaystyle=\Biggl|r^{\lambda(\xi)}\int_{D}\frac{|u|^{-\lambda(\xi)}g(u)}{w-u}\big|{\rm d}^{2}u\big|\Biggr|

\displaystyle\leq r^{\operatorname{Re}(\lambda(\xi))}|g|_{L^{p}_{-2+\delta}}\left(\int_{D}\frac{\big|{\rm d}^{2}u\big|}{|u|^{2-(\delta-\operatorname{Re}(\lambda(\xi)))\frac{p}{p-1}}|w-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}.

(B.4)

We will denote
\delta(\xi)=\delta-\operatorname{Re}(\lambda(\xi))
. By our conditions on
\delta(\xi)
and
p
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\big|{\rm d}^{2}u\big|}{|u|^{2-\delta(\xi)\frac{p}{p-1}}|w-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}<\infty,

so if
D_{1/|w|}
denotes the disk centered at the origin of radius
1/|w|
, we can write

\displaystyle\int_{D}\frac{\big|{\rm d}^{2}u\big|}{|u|^{2-\delta(\xi)\frac{p}{p-1}}|w-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|w|^{(1-\delta(\xi))\frac{p}{p-1}}}\int_{D_{1/|w|}}\frac{\big|{\rm d}^{2}u\big|}{|u|^{2-\delta(\xi)\frac{p}{p-1}}|1-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|w|^{(1-\delta(\xi))\frac{p}{p-1}}}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\big|{\rm d}^{2}u\big|}{|u|^{2-\delta(\xi)\frac{p}{p-1}}|1-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}=\frac{1}{|w|^{(1-\delta(\xi))\frac{p}{p-1}}}c(\xi),

where
c(\xi)\colon U(\xi_{0})\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

c(\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{\big|{\rm d}^{2}u\big|}{|u|^{2-\delta(\xi)\frac{p}{p-1}}|1-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}.

Now it is easy to check that
c(\xi)
depends continuously on
\xi
. To show this, consider the function
h(u)
defined in the following way

\displaystyle h(u)=\frac{1}{|u|^{2-\delta_{0}\frac{p}{p-1}}|1-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}\qquad\text{if}\quad|u|<1,

\displaystyle h(u)=\frac{1}{|u|^{2-\delta_{1}\frac{p}{p-1}}|1-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}\qquad\text{if}\quad|u|>1.

Then we get that
h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{C})
and furthermore

\frac{1}{|u|^{2-\delta(\xi)\frac{p}{p-1}}|1-u|^{\frac{p}{p-1}}}\leq h(u)\qquad\text{for every}\quad\xi\in U(\xi_{0})\quad\text{and for almost every}\quad u\in\mathbb{C}.

From this fact, it follows that
c(\xi)
must be a continuous function of
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
. In particular, by further restricting
U(\xi_{0})
if necessary, we can find a constant
C
such that
c(\xi)\leq C
for
{\xi\in U(\xi_{0})}
. Hence, by going back (
B.4
), we conclude that

|T_{\xi}(g)(w)|\leq|g|_{L^{p}_{-2+\delta}}\frac{|w|^{\operatorname{Re}(\lambda(\xi))}C^{\frac{p-1}{p}}}{|w|^{1-\delta(\xi)}}=|g|_{L^{p}_{-2+\delta}}\frac{C^{\frac{p-1}{p}}}{|w|^{1-\delta}},

so we finally get that

|T_{\xi}(g)|_{C^{0}_{-1+\delta}}\leq|g|_{L^{p}_{-2+\delta}}C^{\frac{p-1}{p}}
for all
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let

\displaystyle U_{\delta}:=\bigl\{u\in\Gamma(D,\operatorname{End}(E))\mid u_{3}\in C^{0}_{2+\delta},\,u_{0}\in C^{0}_{\delta}\bigr\},

\displaystyle A_{\delta}:=\bigl\{a\in\Gamma(D,\operatorname{End}(E))\mid a_{3}\in L^{p}_{\delta+1},\,a_{0}\in L^{p}_{\delta-1}\bigr\}.

Furthermore, let
\overline{\partial}_{0}=\overline{\partial}-\bigl(\xi\overline{m}+\frac{m^{(3)}}{2}\bigr)H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}}
. Then for some
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
there is a continuous map
T_{\xi}\colon A_{\delta^{\prime}}\to U_{\delta^{\prime}}
such that
\overline{\partial}_{0}(T_{\xi}(c))=c
. If we pick
U(\xi_{0})
sufficiently small, the family of solution maps
T_{\xi}
has a uniform bound in
\xi
.

Proof.

This follows from the beginning of the proof of
[
2
, Lemma 7.1]
and our previous Lemma
B.1
.
∎

Theorem B.3
.

For a sufficiently small disk
D_{\lambda}
centered at
w=0
and
U(\xi_{0})
bounded and sufficiently small, we have a solution
g_{\xi}(w)
of the problem described in (
B.1
) that is defined on
{D_{\lambda}\times U(\xi_{0})}
and depends continuously on
\xi
. Furthermore,
g_{\xi}(w)\to 1
uniformly in
\xi
as
{w\to 0}
.

Proof.

We will follow mostly the same argument as in the proof of
[
2
, Lemma 7.1]
. We put it here just to emphasize the behavior in families parametrized by
\xi
, which is not done in the aforementioned paper.

For
\lambda>0
, let
h_{\lambda}
be the homothety
h_{\lambda}(w)=\lambda w
, and let
\varphi(w,\xi)=\exp\bigl(\bigl(\frac{\overline{\xi}}{2w^{2}}-\frac{\xi}{2\overline{w}^{2}}\bigr)H\bigr)
. Furthermore, we denote
c(w,\xi)=-a_{\text{reg}}^{0,1}(w)-\xi\theta_{\text{reg}}^{\dagger_{h}}(w)
.

If we write
g_{\xi}(w)=1+u(w,\xi)
, the problem (
B.1
) that we are trying to solve can be rephrased as the problem of finding
u(w,\xi)
such that

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{0}(u(w,\xi))=\left[\xi H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\overline{w}^{3}},u(w,\xi)\right]+c(w,\xi)(1+u(w,\xi)),\qquad u(0,\xi)=0,

\displaystyle u_{0}\in C^{0}_{\delta^{\prime}},\qquad u_{3}\in C^{0}_{2+\delta^{\prime}}\qquad\text{for}\quad\delta^{\prime}>0\quad\text{as before}.

The last two equations can be satisfied if
u\in U_{\delta^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, as in the proof of
[
2
, Lemma 7.1]
, to solve the first equation is enough to find a fixed point of the map
\widetilde{T}_{\xi}\colon U_{\delta^{\prime}}\to U_{\delta^{\prime}}
given by

\widetilde{T}_{\xi}(u)=h^{*}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\cdot T_{\xi}\big(\big(h^{*}_{\lambda}(\varphi)\big)^{-1}\cdot[h^{*}_{\lambda}(c(\xi))(1+u)]\big),

where “
\cdot
” denotes the action by conjugation. Indeed, we have that if
v
is a fixed point, then

\begin{split}\overline{\partial}_{0}(v)=\overline{\partial}_{0}\bigl(\widetilde{T}_{\xi}(v)\bigr)=\left[\xi H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{w}}{\lambda^{2}\overline{w}^{3}},v\right]+h^{*}_{\lambda}(c)(1+v).\end{split}

Now since
\overline{\partial}_{0}
is invariant under rescaling, if we put
\widetilde{w}=\lambda w
, then
u(\widetilde{w},\xi)=v(\widetilde{w}/\lambda,\xi)
satisfies

\overline{\partial}_{0}(u(\widetilde{w},\xi))=\left[\xi H\frac{{\rm d}\overline{\widetilde{w}}}{\overline{\widetilde{w}}^{3}},u(\widetilde{w},\xi)\right]+c(\widetilde{w},\xi)(1+u(\widetilde{w},\xi)),

and hence
1+u
is the solution we seek. Now to find the fixed point, we need to show that
\widetilde{T}_{\xi}
is a contraction. We have

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{T}_{\xi}(u)-\widetilde{T}_{\xi}(v)\big|_{U_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq C|u-v|_{U_{\delta}}|h^{*}_{\lambda}(c(\xi))|_{A_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq C|u-v|_{U_{\delta}}\bigl(\big|h^{*}_{\lambda}(a_{\text{reg}})^{0,1}\big|_{A_{\delta}}+D\big|h^{*}_{\lambda}\bigl(\theta_{\text{reg}}^{\dagger_{h}}\bigr)\big|_{A_{\delta}}\bigr),

where
C
does not depend on
\xi
; and since
U(\xi_{0})
is bounded, we have the last bound with
D
independent of
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
. Furthermore, by the proof of
[
2
, Lemma 7.1]
, we have that
\smash{\big|h^{*}_{\lambda}(a_{\text{reg}}^{0,1})\big|_{A_{\delta}}}=\smash{\lambda^{\delta}\big|a_{\text{reg}}^{0,1}\big|_{A_{\delta}}}
and
\big|h^{*}_{\lambda}\bigl(\theta^{\dagger_{h}}_{\text{reg}}\bigr)\big|_{A_{\delta}}=\lambda^{\delta}\big|\theta^{\dagger_{h}}_{\text{reg}}\big|_{A_{\delta}}
, so that

\big|\widetilde{T}_{\xi}(u)-\widetilde{T}_{\xi}(v)\big|_{U_{\delta}}\leq\lambda^{\delta}C|u-v|_{U_{\delta}}\bigl(\big|a_{\text{reg}}^{0,1}\big|_{A_{\delta}}+D\big|\theta_{\text{reg}}^{\dagger_{h}}\big|_{A_{\delta}}\bigr).

Hence, for
\lambda
small enough
\widetilde{T}_{\xi}
becomes a contraction, so we can find a fixed point.

Since
\widetilde{T}_{\xi}(u)
is a continuous function of
w
and
\xi
, and the rightmost term in the last inequality does not depend on
\xi
, we actually get that the fixed point must be a continuous function of both variables defined on
D_{\lambda}\times U(\xi_{0})
.

Finally, by shrinking
U(\xi_{0})
and
D_{\lambda}
if necessary, we have that our solution
g_{\xi}(w)=1+u(w,\xi)
to problem (
B.1
) is uniformly continuous on
D_{\lambda}\times U(\xi_{0})
. Hence, given
\epsilon>0
, we can find
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
such that
|g_{\xi}(w)-g_{\xi^{\prime}}(w^{\prime})|<\epsilon
as long as
|w-w^{\prime}|<\delta_{1}
and
|\xi-\xi^{\prime}|<\delta_{2}
. In particular, if
|w|<\delta_{1}
, we have that

|g_{\xi}(w)-1|=|g_{\xi}(w)-g_{\xi}(0)|<\epsilon
for all
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
,
so
g_{\xi}(w)\to 1
as
w\to 0
uniformly in
\xi\in U(\xi_{0})
.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Proposition
3.57

Here we prove the first case of Proposition
3.57
, where
\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{1}(t,\xi))>\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{2}(t,\xi))
, since the other case is similar. We will use the following notation

\lambda_{i}(t,\xi)=-\xi^{-1}\gamma^{*}\theta_{ii}-\xi\gamma^{*}\theta^{\dagger_{h}}_{ii},\qquad\lambda_{ij}(t,\xi)=\lambda_{i}(t,\xi)-\lambda_{j}(t,\xi),\qquad R=-\gamma^{*}A-\xi\gamma^{*}\theta^{\dagger_{h}}_{\text{od}},

where
\theta^{\dagger_{h}}_{\text{od}}
denotes the off-diagonal part of
\theta^{\dagger_{h}}
. If
\gamma^{*}A_{d}
denotes the diagonal part of
\gamma^{*}A
, then after doing a diagonal gauge transformation of the form
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\to(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cdot\exp\bigl(-\int_{a}^{t}\gamma^{*}A_{d}\bigr)
, we can gauge away the diagonal part of
\gamma^{*}A
, so we will assume from the beginning that we are in this gauge. We then have that
R
only has off-diagonal elements, while
\lambda_{i}(t,\xi)
is still the same as before.

The first thing we want to show is that there is a continuous solution to the following integral equation

\displaystyle y_{i}(t,\xi)=\delta_{i2}\exp\left(\int_{a}^{t}\lambda_{2}(\tau,\xi){\rm d}\tau\right)-\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp\left(\int_{\tau}^{t}\lambda_{i}(s,\xi){\rm d}s\right)R_{ij}(\tau,\xi)y_{j}(\tau,\xi){\rm d}\tau

(C.1)

for
i,j=1,2
,
i\neq j
, on any interval
(a_{0},\infty)
, as long as
\xi
is restricted to lie in a small enough neighborhood of
0
. It is easy to check that a solution of (
C.1
), gives a solution to the original flatness equation in our chosen gauge. More explicitly, if we denote
M(t)=\exp\bigl(-\int_{a}^{t}\gamma^{*}A_{d}\bigr)
, then in the frame
(\widetilde{\eta}_{1},\widetilde{\eta}_{2})=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\cdot M
we have a solution of the form
s(t,\xi)=y_{1}(t,\xi)\widetilde{\eta}_{1}+y_{2}(t,\xi)\widetilde{\eta}_{2}
.

If we perform the change

y_{i}(t,\xi)=z_{i}(t,\xi)\exp\left(\int_{a}^{t}\lambda_{2}(\tau,\xi){\rm d}\tau\right),

we obtain the following integral equation for the
z_{i}(t,\xi)
:

\displaystyle z_{i}(t,\xi)=\delta_{i2}-\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp\left(\int_{\tau}^{t}\lambda_{i2}(s,\xi){\rm d}s\right)R_{ij}(\tau,\xi)z_{j}(\tau,\xi){\rm d}\tau

(C.2)

for
i,j=1,2
,
i\neq j
.

If we use the integral relation twice and change the order of integration, we get the following integral equations for the
z_{i}(t,\xi)
:

\displaystyle z_{1}(t,\xi)={}

\displaystyle-\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}\lambda_{21}\right)R_{12}(\tau){\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{\infty}\left(\int_{t}^{s}\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}\lambda_{21}\right)R_{12}(\tau){\rm d}\tau\right)R_{21}(s)z_{1}(s){\rm d}s

\displaystyle:={}

\displaystyle\delta(t,\xi)+\int_{t}^{\infty}\epsilon_{1}(t,s,\xi)z_{1}(s,\xi){\rm d}s

\displaystyle z_{2}(t,\xi)={}

\displaystyle 1+\int_{t}^{\infty}\left(\int_{t}^{s}R_{21}(\tau)\exp\left(\int_{\tau}^{s}\lambda_{21}\right){\rm d}\tau\right)R_{12}(s)z_{2}(s){\rm d}s

\displaystyle:={}

\displaystyle 1+\int_{t}^{\infty}\epsilon_{2}(t,s,\xi)z_{2}(s,\xi){\rm d}s,

(C.3)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\delta(t,\xi):=-\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}\lambda_{21}\right)R_{12}(\tau){\rm d}\tau,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}(t,s,\xi):=\left(\int_{t}^{s}\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}\lambda_{21}\right)R_{12}(\tau){\rm d}\tau\right)R_{21}(s),

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2}(t,s,\xi):=\left(\int_{t}^{s}\exp\left(\int_{\tau}^{s}\lambda_{21}\right)R_{21}(\tau){\rm d}\tau\right)R_{12}(s).

Before showing that there is a solution
z_{i}(t,\xi)
for the integral equations obtained above, we will say a few things about the functions
\delta(t,\xi)
and
\epsilon_{i}(t,s,\xi)
. We will use the following notation:
\lambda_{21}(t,\xi)=\xi^{-1}\lambda_{21,\xi^{-1}}+\xi\lambda_{21,\xi}
, where
\lambda_{21,\xi^{-1}}=\gamma^{*}(-\theta_{22}+\theta_{11})
and
\lambda_{21,\xi}=\gamma^{*}\bigl(-\theta^{\dagger_{h}}_{22}+\theta^{\dagger_{h}}_{11}\bigr)
.

Notice that by our choice of gauge and WKB path, the term
\lambda_{21,\xi^{-1}}
is constant. Hence, after integration by parts, we find the following expression for
\delta(t,\xi)
:

\displaystyle\delta(t,\xi)={}

\displaystyle\frac{\xi}{{\lambda}_{21,\xi^{-1}}}R_{12}(t,\xi)+\frac{\xi}{\lambda_{21,\xi^{-1}}}\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}\xi^{-1}\lambda_{21,\xi^{-1}}{\rm d}s\right)\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\tau}

\displaystyle\times\left(\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}\xi\lambda_{21,\xi}(s){\rm d}s\right)R_{12}(\tau,\xi)\right){\rm d}\tau,

(C.4)

where we used the fact that the elements of
R
go to
0
(exponentially fast) as
t\to\infty
.
Hence, using this exponential decrease of the terms of
R
, we conclude that
|\delta(t,\xi)|\leq|\xi|f(t,\xi)
, where
f(t,\xi)\to 0
as
t\to\infty
uniformly in
\xi
for small enough
\xi
(restricted to the corresponding half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{m}
). Furthermore, for fixed
t
, we have
\delta(t,\xi)\to 0
as
\xi\to 0
with
\xi\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
.

Similarly, we have the following expressions for
\epsilon_{i}
after integration by parts:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}(t,s,\xi)={}

\displaystyle\xi\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}\lambda_{21}\right)\frac{R_{12}(\tau,\xi)}{\lambda_{21,\xi^{-1}}}\Bigg|_{\tau=t}^{\tau=s}R_{21}(s,\xi)
